> ## SCENE 7 — The Police Station / Jean Luc / The Envelope
> 
> Then Jean Luc entered our lives. Having once reported my stolen bike at the police station, the tedious ritual of filing a complaint was still alive in my mind. It was only because Djamela didn't want to go alone, especially to Ave de Bobozo police station, fearing that she might not get out quickly.
> 
> It was Djamela that was next to me, but in reality Chantal had already taken over her — with a new pair of unnaturally blue jeans and a blouse with a designer logo that looked like a blatant counterfeit. In truth she was trying to be one of them. She even had an air of superiority she had disguised herself in. Her back arched upwards and her chin high. Chantal looked more ruthless compared to D'jamela. Although a sense of disappointment had set in her by the looks of her eyes and tired face. She had lost most of her pre-conceived hopes of a rags-to-riches life that Jean Luc was to give her. She had wanted so much to be like the ghost kids, those of Rue Saint Michel. *À notre belle patrie.*
> 
> Throngs of people filled the police station with sighs of delayed justice. The smell of dusty benches worn out by the sliding buttocks of motionless police due process. The biodegradable odour mixed with the tiredness of the officers coming off the shift almost crippled the atmosphere.
> 
> Other appellants trying to state their cases.
> 
> "He didn't — it was stolen. He bought it off the internet legitimately," a mother pleading on behalf of her sixteen-year-old boy who was in possession of a stolen phone.
> 
> Another woman, wearing a tight black dress, whose mascara was being washed by fake tears, stressing a point to another officer in the background.
> 
> "It's not prostitution, it's escort services. We even have cards with the company number," she pleaded with wet alluring eyes to the officer.
> 
> "More like your pimp's number," answered the officer she was pleading to.
> 
> Then came our turn. The policeman, a young man that looked as though he hadn't had much experience with a QWERTY keyboard, was one-handedly poking the typewriter, whilst listening to the row of people lined up. He attended them at the same rate as his typing speed.
> 
> He looked up and saw Djamela's face, but couldn't help himself from making a grunting sound. "Mhh — what do we have here," he said.
> 
> Djamela looked at me and motioned me to go forward.
> 
> "She's reporting a hit and run," I said, gesturing the officer toward her.
> 
> "So why are you talking if she's the one who's reporting?" he said.
> 
> "You sound like you know what happened more than she does," he said, gesturing and winking at me.
> 
> "No, it's not like that," I looked at him seriously, as though D'jamela was my sister.
> 
> "Where was the location, what type of car, and who did the running?" he said, resuming his one-handed typing as if we were wasting his time.
> 
> "Look at my socials. My status," said Djamela, holding out her phone as though it were some precious tablet bearing vital inscriptions.
> 
> The officer looked at the picture of Jean Luc holding D'jamela in his arms behind a G-Wagon Mercedes Benz.
> 
> "We deal with hard evidence. Not AI or phantom ghosts," he said to D'jamela.
> 
> Having exhausted all the pleading to the officer and witnessing his blatant indifference, she leaned towards him, but backed away after flinching at the smell of his onion-whiskey breath.
> 
> "My menstrual cycle. There's a latency of more than a month," she said.
> 
> The change in the policeman's face was immediate, as if something had changed the air.
> 
> From the foreground came an older policeman, who had a protruding gut testament to a lifetime of consuming bribes and whiskey.
> 
> "The penal code. A teenage minor. Rape. Thirty years. And twelve lashes on top of that," said the older officer as though he were reading from a pre-written script.
> 
> The thought of someone like Jean Luc being jailed was a blessing in disguise. It made me think of how D'jamela would be free from his evil clutches.
> 
> "A mere buccal swab will suffice as conclusive evidence," the older officer said.
> 
> "Shame to the predator, for alluring young girls," he continued, as though preaching at St. Augustine's parish.
> 
> Whilst the younger officer was still poking away at the typewriter, he looked up at D'jamela and quietly whispered, as if he wanted to finish off Jean Luc:
> 
> "Where is his number. We will call him now. Summon him to come."
> 
> "Yes, we will finish the case — if you understand me," he said, assuring D'jamela that justice had already taken place.
> 
> D'jamela took out her phone and handed it to the officer, whilst dialling Jean Luc's number.
> 
> A cold acidic taste entered my mouth. My blood pressure had already risen. The broken fan above didn't make it easier as things began to heat up.
> 
> It only took twenty minutes for Jean Luc to arrive at the station.
> 
> He came in with all airs, as if he had prepared for the situation. Underneath his Congolese suit, he had a fat envelope that seemed to be the object of attention of the police officer as he took it out.
> 
> The officers became more relaxed and took Jean Luc and Djamela to a room at the back of the station.
> 
> It didn't take long for them to come out. The officers looked happily at each other as though they had won the lottery. Djamela's head and back were bent down in a stoop, as if something demeaning had happened to her.
> 
> Djamela held the brown envelope that Jean Luc had brought with him — although its contents had depleted along the way between going to and from the backroom. Making it look thinner.
> 
> > [!AI] Questions for this scene
> > 1. "Chantal had already taken over her" — this is one of the saddest lines in the draft. Djamela has already lost herself before she's lost. Can this observation be expanded slightly — one more physical detail of what Daudi sees that isn't Djamela anymore?
> > 2. The police station waiting room — stolen phone, escort services, one-fingered typing — functions as a portrait of institutional rot. Compare this to the opening of *Immaculate* by Marlon James. Is it achieving the same satirical weight, or is it moving too fast?
> > 3. Djamela's pregnancy reveal to the officer ("my menstrual cycle... a latency of more than a month") is devastating and matter-of-fact. Does Daudi react to this in the text? He should. What does he feel that he doesn't say?
> > 4. Jean Luc arrives in twenty minutes — this means he was nearby, probably following them, already prepared. Should Daudi notice this? The speed of his arrival is itself evidence of something.
> > 5. The fat envelope becoming a thin envelope after the backroom — this is the story's central betrayal shown in one image. Is it clear enough, or does it need one more sentence of Daudi processing what he's seeing?
> > 6. The scene ends on the thin envelope. Where does it go next? What does Djamela say, if anything? What does Daudi do? The draft cuts here — what is being avoided, and should it be faced?

> 
> ## STRUCTURAL NOTES
> 
> > [!AI] Big Picture Questions
> > 1. The story is told entirely in retrospect — Daudi already knows Djamela is dead. How do we feel the weight of that knowledge without it becoming elegiac too early? Where should the retrospective voice be strongest?
> > 2. The narrative question is: who killed Djamela, and will Daudi be able to do what the police won't? But the deeper question is: did Daudi see the signs and look away? Which question should the story foreground?
> > 3. There are three men who could be responsible or implicated — Jean Luc, Morisho, the unnamed man at the lakeside hotel (the fob key). How do we calibrate their levels of suspicion across the story?
> > 4. Djamela is the centre of the story but is only ever seen through Daudi's eyes. What are we not seeing? What does Djamela know that Daudi doesn't? Is there a scene that gives us a glimpse of her interiority that the current draft is missing?
> > 5. The vaccination scar detail ("usually done six months before giving birth") implies Djamela may have been pregnant before — or that someone else was. What is the intended reading?
> > 6. Entropy is the story's governing metaphor: things fall apart, the lake remembers everything. At the end, does entropy win, or does something hold? What is the story's final position on this?
> > 7. The story is set in Kalemie, DRC — a specific place with a specific colonial history (Lake Tanganyika, Belgian Congo, mineral extraction). How much of this should be made explicit? What is the risk of it becoming backdrop vs. structure?
> > 8. Compared to the Africa Commonwealth Prize winners (Emezi, Ilo, Ngamije) — all of which use African language code-switching as a deliberate political act — this story uses Swahili, French, and Lingala similarly. Is the current balance right, or is there a case for more untranslated language left to stand on its own?

> ## OPENING — New Version (Option C)
> 
> Port de Kalemie. The air thickly filled with an infusion of colonial coffee and rotten carcasses hidden under the guise of mineral mines, full of corpses aged twelve to seventeen, yet not much younger than me.
> 
> In our neighbourhood they called me *the founder of dead things*.
> 
> That morning I knew something was wrong before I knew what it was. The way you know when a room has fewer people in it than it should. The dala dala was not there. Morisho's voice — *mia tano mia tano* — was not there. Just the boda bodas idling in the wrong silence, and a small crowd with their backs turned to something by the lakeshore road.
> 
> I asked one of them, a boy not much older than me, where Morisho was.
> 
> He wouldn't meet my eyes.
> 
> *"Wamemchukua."* He said it low, his chin tilting toward the police truck parked crooked at the edge of the road. *"Polisi wamemchukua Morisho asubuhi. Wanasema anahusika na kifo cha msichana."*
> 
> The ground did something strange under my feet. The same thing it had done the day I found the antelope skull in the bush — a small, private collapse nobody else could see.
> 
> *"Msichana gani?"*
> 
> He shrugged. The way people shrug when they already know but don't want to be the first to say the name out loud.
> 
> Standing there in the morning light that smelled of lake and dagaa and wrong, I began arranging the things I already knew. The way I arranged the things on my bedroom wall — the motherboard, the hubcap, the antelope skull. Found things. Things that told you something had happened before you arrived.
> 
> Her fingernails, meticulously polished every Friday and removed every Monday before school.
> 
> The small faded vaccination scar near her elbow, not the upper arm — not where they usually put them. Vaccinations like that are usually done six months before giving birth.
> 
> The smell — somewhere at the back of my mind, the name of it refusing to come — of a cologne mostly used by Congolese men.
> 
> The fob shaped like a cartoon lion in her left pocket. The door access card attached to it. The kind that opens expensive apartment buildings. The kind found at the lakeside hotel.
> 
> The last image on her phone: a blurry selfie tagged *#blessed*, and at the edge of the frame, a man's hand on her shoulder, a gold ring on his finger.
> 
> And the last thing. The thing I kept coming back to.
> 
> She had written my name — *Daudi* — in the dust on the inside of her school locker the week before she left. And erased it before walking out for the final time.
> 
> D'jamela's father had once told me: *some women resemble the lake.*
> 
> I was beginning to understand what he meant.
>

> ## SCENE 1 — D'juma's Words / The Lake
> 
> D'jamela's father D'juma's words have rung in my mind like a school bell. Although incomprehensible to me at first, yet I'm beginning to understand them as the days go by.
> 
> "Some women resemble the lake." He said.
> 
> "Daudi the lake is cold just like some women. Many people come and go, yet it remains the same. Dark, deep and keeps swallowing things down." He said.
> 
> "And knows more secrets than anybody." He said.
> 
> I realised how the lake pulls things down whilst engaged in the futile pastime of skimming pebbles on the lake surface. A temporal experiment of gravity. Pleasurable until the 5th or 6th throws, right where boredom starts to set in and it becomes clear that gravity does exist. But I had yet to experience how a woman would pull you in like the lake.
> 
> The thoughts of holding things together was the subject of our physics class on entropy. Yet it made me wonder, whether things were held together or pulled like those stones at the bottom of the lake.
>

> > 3. "I had yet to experience how a woman would pull you in like the lake" — this is retrospective knowledge. Does it belong here or does it belong at the end, after everything has happened?
> > 4. Entropy appears for the first time here through physics class. How do we make sure entropy becomes a genuine structural motif rather than a clever idea that gets forgotten?
> > 5. Should D'juma know at this point that Djamela is already in trouble? What does it change if he does?

> ## SCENE 2 — The Neighbourhood / Djamela's Introduction
> 
> The down part of town is where we resided. Thronged fishermen and boda boda drivers looking for an easy catch. A lively population of a mixture of the people of the lake who seem to wander throughout the countries adjacent to the lake. The lake seemed to accord immunity from any passports or visa restriction, you seldom knew which person came from which country. Things like someone's pointed nose, or how short or tall, or if you believed in Jesus didn't matter to someone who came from Kalemie.
> 
> Djamela took her mother's figure, she was tall and beautiful just like her mother.
> 
> D'jamela is probably the closest person that I have been next to, besides my mother. Who had cared and caressed me during infancy and childhood.
>

> ## SCENE 3 — The Dala Dala / Morisho / The Ghost Kids
> 
> The last time me and Djamela were together was on a dala dala, a 16-seater Japanese Toyota Mini Hiace. A cheap and affordable favoured transport for the locals. The conductor, a school dropout, called Morisho.
> 
> "Mia tano mia tano." He continued using his skill of managing 30 passengers already in the vehicle, and yet managed to magically lure more in.
> 
> There would be occasional brushes with teenage school girls, which often led to their skirts stained with a frothy fluid of sorts on the back of their skirts.
> 
> "Mototo muzuri, soma shule usije ukawa konda kama mimi," said Morisho.
> 
> "Ndeko, chukua hii elfu mbili ukatumie shule," he said as he took out a banknote from his stack of notes he had neatly arranged in the side of his draws.
> 
> Anitha took the note, and hid it inside her bra, as though it was a treasure to be protected. She said nothing but coyly looked at him as a thirteen-year-old school girl would who thought she was being given something precious. Anitha dropped off at the Ecole Secondaire de Kitumaini stop as she usually did before seven thirty, but today she was early as it was her class that was cleaning the school yard according to the timetable.
> 
> As Anitha got off the dala dala, her best friend Djamela followed her with eyes of envy.
> 
> "She's eating chips kuku today," said Djamela.
> 
> "The chicken and chips will be digging her own grave," I whispered to D'jamela. And added "HIV," motioning my eyes to Morisho.
> 
> A Toyota Prado SUV parked beside us on the traffic lights stop on the double road along Ave de Industrielle.
> 
> "Look. Look at them," said D'jamela excitedly in haste, before the lights turned green.
> 
> "The way they wear those checkered skirts with their ties and blazers," she said, pointing at the school children quietly sitting at the back of the vehicle.
> 
> "They go to Monseigneur André Kaseba Catholic School," I told her, pointing to their checkered uniform.
> 
> "My mother said it's the most expensive school in town," I said like I knew everything about the school.
> 
> I had always thought about how we hardly met up with them on the dala dalas, and how maybe through entropy or gravity, we might be pulled together with them.
> 
> "Once they entered their parent's SUVs they went to their school, and then entered their red clay roofed mansions guarded by gates and security guards," said Morisho.
> 
> "Those children are ghosts to you, you will never see them nor talk to them," he said in a clear voice, making sure it sank deep in our hearts.
> 
> The ghost kids weren't the subject of envy, especially on my part. But I envied the way they were secured and safe, living in luxury. Probably eating European things like baguettes, pizzas and burgers, everything European. Not the ugali dagaa from our school.
>

> > 2. "The ghost kids" motif is strong. Djamela wanting to be one of them drives the whole story. Is there one more detail we can add here — something Djamela says or does — that makes her desire more specific and more heartbreaking?
> > 3. Daudi uses "HIV" as a warning. Does this land correctly — teenage boy, shorthand, protective — or does it need unpacking?
> > 4. Morisho's "those children are ghosts to you" is one of the story's thesis lines. Should it be set apart somehow — a paragraph break, a beat of silence from Daudi — or does the current flow work?
> > 5. "Through entropy or gravity, we might be pulled together with them" — Daudi applying physics to class. Is this too neat or does it feel like how this specific boy thinks?

> ## SCENE 4 — Djamela's Birthday / The Watermelon
> 
> Anitha was first to turn thirteen, barely three months before D'jamela's birthday, which we had celebrated a few weeks ago.
> 
> My first lesson on how women can pull things happened when D'jamela turned thirteen. As she made her wish and blew the candle.
> 
> The watermelon supposedly symbolizing the birthday cake.
> 
> "Baba surely you could have gotten me a cake, even a cheap one," said D'jamela.
> 
> D'jamela's father worked at the fruit market in Ave de Tanganyique for most of the mornings, before handing the stall to her mother in the evening whilst he attended his traditional African healing customers.
> 
> "Watermelons are more, healthier and natural," said her father D'juma.
> 
> "Besides it's more African. And we don't have to celebrate birthdays with cakes, that's a western thing," he said.
> 
> "Look at it this way — watermelons are sweet like you D'jamela, they symbolize the natural element of sweetness and love," said her mother, trying to convince D'jamela of the truth of her father's words. "Bolingo," she added, pressing the word flat against the melon's green skin like a seal.
> 
> "Make a wish and blow," said her father D'juma. His own lips moved first, a private "Nzambe" under his breath, as if he were making a wish of his own before he'd hand her the chance to make hers.
> 
> "To be Kim Kardashian, amen," said D'jamela.
> 
> "What is Kim Kardashian?" asked D'juma.
> 
> "A social media influencer," said D'jamela's mother quickly, attempting to disregard the name.
> 
> With her eyes closed as if culminating a ritual, she blew the candle. The smoke partially rose up gathering into a dark cloud just above the shiny corrugated iron roof. It coagulated, like Leopold never wanting to leave Brazzaville. She handed me a piece of the red watermelon, and in those moments of devouring its sweet juice, trying to bite off every inch of it, down to its bare green skin. It tasted like it was preserving itself in sugar.
> 
> To me it felt as though it was Djamela's father's desperate attempt to stave off the inevitable rot of the whole situation.
> 
> Later, on the porch, we looked out at the Lake. With its rusted colonial ships.
> 
> "He thinks I want things," she said, in a low and steady voice.
> 
> "I want to control things; I want to conquer like the Europeans."
> 
> When we were children, six or seven years old, she told me that the lake was a mirror:
> 
> "If you stare long enough, you can see your own death," she told me, looking steadily with her eyes directed to the farthest point of the lake.
> 
> "Don't you see Daudi, we can beat them at their own game. No nuclear weapons or school degrees, just followers," she continued.
> 
> > [!AI] Questions for this scene
> > 1. "Like Leopold never wanting to leave Brazzaville" — this is the story's most explicit colonial reference. Does it land or does it feel like it's reaching? Should it be unpacked slightly or left as is for the reader who gets it?
> > 2. D'juma refuses a cake because "that's a western thing" — and then Djamela wishes to be Kim Kardashian. This irony is the whole story in miniature. Is it too neat? Or is neatness exactly right for a birthday scene?
> > 3. "To be Kim Kardashian, amen" — the "amen" is doing a lot of work (birthday wish as prayer, aspiration as religion). Should Daudi notice this? Comment on it? Or leave it unremarked?
> > 4. "If you stare long enough, you can see your own death" — Djamela says this at age six or seven, and she's right. How do we handle this line without making Djamela feel like a prophecy rather than a person?
> > 5. "I want to conquer like the Europeans / just followers" — Djamela's ambition is stated directly. Is this too explicit for her character, or does the directness fit a thirteen-year-old girl who hasn't learned yet to disguise what she wants?
> > 6. D'juma as traditional healer, fruit seller, and philosopher — is he fully realized enough? What one additional detail would make him three-dimensional?
>

> ## SCENE 5 — The Dala Dala Continued / The Dagaa Stop / Jean Luc Appears
> 
> An empty stomach, the strong smell of the freshly fried dagaa from mama ntilie vendors brought me back to the moment. Realising we had reached our stop.
> 
> "They call them anchovy," said D'jamela. She always made things seem more chic using trendy European words.
> 
> I nodded in return.
> 
> "You mean the dagaa," I said, pointing out the obvious.
> 
> "Shusha konda," I said, banging the side of the minivan to signal the driver to stop at the next stop.
> 
> "Sawa shemeji," said Morisho, giving Djamela a five-hundred-shilling coin.
> 
> "Ya kwako hiyo shemeji yangu," said Morisho. "Keba," he added, quieter, almost lost under the engine — a word thrown after her like a hand that couldn't quite reach.
> 
> As we were about to disembark, the boda bodas started greeting us with their horns. It was as though they were awaiting our arrival.
> 
> — Mambo mrembo
> 
> — Mototo wa mujini.
> 
> — Mototo muzuri.
> 
> — Kitoko mingi.
> 
> They all said whilst gesturing at Djamela's beauty.
> 
> Usually my voice would be squirmish trying to respond to their unwanted comments, but then something stopped me.
> 
> A man gestured to me from the other side of the tinted glass of a dark black SUV, like the ones the ghost kids from Monseigneur André Kaseba Catholic School used. We pretended not to notice him and kept on walking.
> 
> His horn blazed in our direction, a different tune to the boda bodas and dala dalas. The air suddenly changed from roasted dagaa from the fresh lake catch to perfumes from Dubai as he lowered the windows with a swish — not the usual clink.
> 
> The car radio blazed with Fally Ipupa sliding into its sebene, guitars breaking loose from the melody, an atalaku somewhere underneath it all barking names into the rhythm. The man lowered the window with a quiet swish. "Wow, just look at that BMW X5," said Djamela, as we made a U-turn from the school direction and started looking at the beast. In between the beats of Fally Ipupa a sound emitted from the interior:
> 
> "Je m'appelle Jean Luc," said the man.
> 
> "Tu es d'où, ma belle?" said the man in his mixed French and Lingala.
> 
> "Rue Saint Michel," answered Djamela, with a different flavour to her voice.
> 
> The ghost children came from Rue Saint Michel. They wore the checkered uniforms. Not like us — we came from Mihogoni or Manzese. Not Rue Saint Michel.
> 
> "Mama alisema," I started to say, visualizing a script taught to us at our Sunday school at St. Augustine Parish along Ave Kabanyo Mute.
> 
> "We shouldn't talk to strangers," said Jean Luc, as though he had also learned the same from Sunday school.
> 
> As he made the dépassement with his black beast on the curb and drew us into his world of leather upholstery and smells of colognes from Dubai:
> 
> "Le contraire, mon garçon."
> 
> "Directeur. Le consul général de Belgique," he said.
> 
> Listening to his broken lines of French and street Congolese Lingala.
> 
> "Ask the boda bodas — they know the town like a Google map," I pointed out to him.
> 
> "Le petit garçon," he called me. His smell of cologne and leather upholstery didn't deter me from continuing my policy of not talking to strangers.
> 
> Remembering the words of my Sunday school teacher at St. Augustine Parish:
> 
> *"Many men bring ruin to young school girls and abandonment of dreams."*
> 
> D'jamela should have listened to me, but she continued the dialogue with the man who called himself Jean Luc.
> 
> He took out a piece of paper and wrote down his phone number and covered a ten thousand shillings note with it.
> 
> Djamela's eyes widened. It wasn't the ten thousand shillings note that astonished her. It could have been the SUV. The fact that she had wanted to be somebody, like those kids that rode in SUVs. Like Kim Kardashian.
> 
> "Je m'appelle?" he asked Djamela.
> 
> "Chantal," she said, as her eyes widened with her body emitting a new frequency, a new signature vibe.
>

> ## SCENE 6 — The Rain / The Mattress / The Twenty Seconds
> 
> The look in D'jamela's eyes reminded me of the incident when we lived near Marché le Lubuye. The time we shared a moment in the rain. A cordial event compared to what was happening now.
> 
> That sweet aroma of damp clothing infused with perfumed khangas filled the air of my lowly dwellings. It felt as if all life existed in the confines of those four walls of baked bricks. The half-soaked dark brown foam mattress laid out on the floor. The two-foot sealed window provided little if no lighting. Yet D'jamela shone with an aura that was unforgettable, as she stood at my bedroom door, running from the rain.
> 
> The droplets seeping through the tin roof bounced off her shoulders. The rest collected itself in the bucket I had meticulously positioned.
> 
> "Come in D'jamela," I said to her, cautioning her not to kick the buckets. I motioned her to the only sensible part of the room, a dry patch of the brown mattress.
> 
> "Do you understand entropy?" she posed the question, looking at the papers spread out on the mattress along with the half-torn physics book spread out on the grey sheets that covered the mattress. The sheets devoid of the fresh smell of detergent and smeared with patches of evidence of dreams and nightmares.
> 
> "It's a natural state," I said, trying to sound like my everyday vocabulary.
> 
> "Order requires energy; disorder is its default," I told her.
> 
> "It's like holding on to something, and once unbearable you have to let go," said Djamela.
> 
> "My father says the Lake is the opposite. He says it puts things back to order. It remembers everything in its right place," she continued.
> 
> "Your father sounds like a wise man, we could learn a lot from him," I said to her, looking for signs of a reaction. I saw none.
> 
> "It's a fusion of Hydrogen and Oxygen. It doesn't have any intelligence," I continued, using my new-found vocabulary.
> 
> Moments of pauses passed by. Then it came without warning, a surprise that scattered the notes on entropy and the physics book placed on the mattress.
> 
> She knelt down on the mattress, sat next to me with her feet touching a wet patch of floor now laminated with droplets of rain that bounced off the edge of the bucket.
> 
> Her eyes flowed, yet restful and inattentive to everything but the space between us.
> 
> "Then what do you want to remember, Mr. Scientist? Encrypted data? Binary codes?" A pause, small and complete. "Motema." Then, softer still: "Or just a colourful moment without meaning?" she said.
> 
> Sitting there in those few moments seemed like a dream that should never have ended. The edge of my knee had accidentally touched her left hand thumb that supported the weight of her body. My mind told me that she felt it and had wanted me to do something. A soft touch, a tender word.
> 
> Not a word came out of my mouth but silence.
> 
> *Tell her to go home, it was late. This isn't right, you shouldn't be here. Do you want me to touch you?*
> 
> With all the premeditation and calculations. Nothing happened except motionless head chatter.
> 
> Then that unpredicted glow from her eyes happened, the bioluminescent stream of pure everything.
> 
> The air, along with the perfumed wet kangas, now had an added aura of a minty after-smell of PK bubble gum and lip gloss, with the darkness of the room staring at us. Lost in the stream of a subliminal moment, things dictating themselves as if they had life in them.
> 
> Tangent and sweet it felt at first. Then perfect and seamless, as if awaiting a timestamp. Right there in the midst of inconsistent heartbeats.
> 
> It took only twenty seconds.
> 
> I gasped for air.
> 
> Things went back to normal, with a heart-breaking realisation of the *désvu* that took place.
> 
> She pulled away. Her breath, short and sharp. The glow faded from her eyes, replaced by a sudden, vulnerable uncertainty.
> 
> She looked at me as if she had revealed the secret backdoor to the universe.
> 
> "See?" she whispered, her voice barely audible.
> 
> "Not everything falls apart."
> 
> She was wrong, of course. Entropy always wins. But in that moment — in the dusty rainy moment of obliviousness — it felt as though she was right.
> 
> It was the most beautiful and catastrophic twenty seconds of my life.
> 
> > [!AI] Questions for this scene
> > 1. This is the story's emotional core. But it's buried deep. Should it be moved earlier — closer to the opening — so the reader knows what Daudi has lost before they see him losing it?
> > 2. "The bioluminescent stream of pure everything" — this is the story reaching for something beyond its usual register. Does it earn it, or is it overwritten?
> > 3. Daudi's internal monologue — *tell her to go home, do you want me to touch you* — is in italics, dissociated. Good. But does it need one more line that shows his failure is not just passivity but fear? What is he actually afraid of here?
> > 4. "Not everything falls apart" / "Entropy always wins" — this is the story's thematic thesis in two lines. Does it work as the payoff of the entropy motif, or does it feel too stated?
> > 5. The twenty seconds appears twice in the draft — here and in the Jean Luc scene (Djamela's eyes widening the same way). Is this echo intentional and strong enough? How can it be sharpened?
> > 6. After the kiss Djamela says "see?" — as if she has proved something. What has she proved? To herself? To Daudi? Should Daudi wonder about this, or leave it as a mystery?
> > 7. New: "Motema" (heart) breaks through her own scientific deflection mid-sentence — one word, in her own tongue, before she covers it back up with the joke about "a colourful moment without meaning." Does this land as the one true thing she says in the whole scene, or does it need to be cut for being too on-the-nose?

> ## SCENE 7 — The Police Station / Jean Luc / The Envelope
> 
> Then Jean Luc entered our lives. Having once reported my stolen bike at the police station, the tedious ritual of filing a complaint was still alive in my mind. It was only because Djamela didn't want to go alone, especially to Ave de Bobozo police station, fearing that she might not get out quickly.
> 
> It was Djamela that was next to me, but in reality Chantal had already taken over her — with a new pair of unnaturally blue jeans and a blouse with a designer logo that looked like a blatant counterfeit. In truth she was trying to be one of them. She even had an air of superiority she had disguised herself in. Her back arched upwards and her chin high. Chantal looked more ruthless compared to D'jamela. Although a sense of disappointment had set in her by the looks of her eyes and tired face. She had lost most of her pre-conceived hopes of a rags-to-riches life that Jean Luc was to give her. She had wanted so much to be like the ghost kids, those of Rue Saint Michel. *À notre belle patrie.*
> 
> Throngs of people filled the police station with sighs of delayed justice. The smell of dusty benches worn out by the sliding buttocks of motionless police due process. The biodegradable odour mixed with the tiredness of the officers coming off the shift almost crippled the atmosphere. Someone near the back benches murmured "Ezali makasi" to no one, and no one answered, because everyone already agreed.
> 
> Other appellants trying to state their cases.
> 
> "He didn't — it was stolen. He bought it off the internet legitimately," a mother pleading on behalf of her sixteen-year-old boy who was in possession of a stolen phone.
> 
> Another woman, wearing a tight black dress, whose mascara was being washed by fake tears, stressing a point to another officer in the background.
> 
> "It's not prostitution, it's escort services. We even have cards with the company number," she pleaded with wet alluring eyes to the officer.
> 
> "More like your pimp's number," answered the officer she was pleading to.
> 
> Then came our turn. The policeman, a young man that looked as though he hadn't had much experience with a QWERTY keyboard, was one-handedly poking the typewriter, whilst listening to the row of people lined up. He attended them at the same rate as his typing speed.
> 
> He looked up and saw Djamela's face, but couldn't help himself from making a grunting sound. "Mhh — what do we have here," he said.
> 
> Djamela looked at me and motioned me to go forward.
> 
> "She's reporting a hit and run," I said, gesturing the officer toward her.
> 
> "So why are you talking if she's the one who's reporting?" he said.
> 
> "You sound like you know what happened more than she does," he said, gesturing and winking at me.
> 
> "No, it's not like that," I looked at him seriously, as though D'jamela was my sister.
> 
> "Where was the location, what type of car, and who did the running?" he said, resuming his one-handed typing as if we were wasting his time.
> 
> "Look at my socials. My status," said Djamela, holding out her phone as though it were some precious tablet bearing vital inscriptions.
> 
> The officer looked at the picture of Jean Luc holding D'jamela in his arms behind a G-Wagon Mercedes Benz.
> 
> "We deal with hard evidence. Not AI or phantom ghosts," he said to D'jamela.
> 
> Having exhausted all the pleading to the officer and witnessing his blatant indifference, she leaned towards him, but backed away after flinching at the smell of his onion-whiskey breath.
> 
> "My menstrual cycle. There's a latency of more than a month," she said.
> 
> The change in the policeman's face was immediate, as if something had changed the air.
> 
> From the foreground came an older policeman, who had a protruding gut testament to a lifetime of consuming bribes and whiskey.
> 
> "The penal code. A teenage minor. Rape. Thirty years. And twelve lashes on top of that," said the older officer as though he were reading from a pre-written script.
> 
> The thought of someone like Jean Luc being jailed was a blessing in disguise. It made me think of how D'jamela would be free from his evil clutches.
> 
> "A mere buccal swab will suffice as conclusive evidence," the older officer said.
> 
> "Shame to the predator, for alluring young girls," he continued, as though preaching at St. Augustine's parish.
> 
> Whilst the younger officer was still poking away at the typewriter, he looked up at D'jamela and quietly whispered, as if he wanted to finish off Jean Luc:
> 
> "Where is his number. We will call him now. Summon him to come."
> 
> "Yes, we will finish the case — if you understand me," he said, assuring D'jamela that justice had already taken place.
> 
> D'jamela took out her phone and handed it to the officer, whilst dialling Jean Luc's number.
> 
> A cold acidic taste entered my mouth. My blood pressure had already risen. The broken fan above didn't make it easier as things began to heat up.
> 
> It only took twenty minutes for Jean Luc to arrive at the station.
> 
> He came in with all airs, as if he had prepared for the situation. Underneath his Congolese suit, he had a fat envelope that seemed to be the object of attention of the police officer as he took it out.
> 
> The officers became more relaxed and took Jean Luc and Djamela to a room at the back of the station.
> 
> It didn't take long for them to come out. The officers looked happily at each other as though they had won the lottery. Djamela's head and back were bent down in a stoop, as if something demeaning had happened to her.
> 
> Djamela held the brown envelope that Jean Luc had brought with him — although its contents had depleted along the way between going to and from the backroom. Making it look thinner.
> 
> > [!AI] Questions for this scene
> > 1. "Chantal had already taken over her" — this is one of the saddest lines in the draft. Djamela has already lost herself before she's lost. Can this observation be expanded slightly — one more physical detail of what Daudi sees that isn't Djamela anymore?
> > 2. The police station waiting room — stolen phone, escort services, one-fingered typing — functions as a portrait of institutional rot. Compare this to the opening of *Immaculate* by Marlon James. Is it achieving the same satirical weight, or is it moving too fast?
> > 3. Djamela's pregnancy reveal to the officer ("my menstrual cycle... a latency of more than a month") is devastating and matter-of-fact. Does Daudi react to this in the text? He should. What does he feel that he doesn't say?
> > 4. Jean Luc arrives in twenty minutes — this means he was nearby, probably following them, already prepared. Should Daudi notice this? The speed of his arrival is itself evidence of something.
> > 5. The fat envelope becoming a thin envelope after the backroom — this is the story's central betrayal shown in one image. Is it clear enough, or does it need one more sentence of Daudi processing what he's seeing?
> > 6. The scene ends on the thin envelope. Where does it go next? What does Djamela say, if anything? What does Daudi do? The draft cuts here — what is being avoided, and should it be faced?
> > 7. New: "Ezali makasi" murmured in the waiting room — does this earn its place as ambient texture, or is it decorative? Should more of the waiting-room dread be carried this way, in overheard language, instead of Daudi's own description?
> 
> ---
> 
> ## STRUCTURAL NOTES
> 
> > [!AI] Big Picture Questions
> > 1. The story is told entirely in retrospect — Daudi already knows Djamela is dead. How do we feel the weight of that knowledge without it becoming elegiac too early? Where should the retrospective voice be strongest?
> > 2. The narrative question is: who killed Djamela, and will Daudi be able to do what the police won't? But the deeper question is: did Daudi see the signs and look away? Which question should the story foreground?
> > 3. There are three men who could be responsible or implicated — Jean Luc, Morisho, the unnamed man at the lakeside hotel (the fob key). How do we calibrate their levels of suspicion across the story?
> > 4. Djamela is the centre of the story but is only ever seen through Daudi's eyes. What are we not seeing? What does Djamela know that Daudi doesn't? Is there a scene that gives us a glimpse of her interiority that the current draft is missing?
> > 5. The vaccination scar detail ("usually done six months before giving birth") implies Djamela may have been pregnant before — or that someone else was. What is the intended reading?
> > 6. Entropy is the story's governing metaphor: things fall apart, the lake remembers everything. At the end, does entropy win, or does something hold? What is the story's final position on this?
> > 7. The story is set in Kalemie, DRC — a specific place with a specific colonial history (Lake Tanganyika, Belgian Congo, mineral extraction). How much of this should be made explicit? What is the risk of it becoming backdrop vs. structure?
> > 8. Compared to the Africa Commonwealth Prize winners (Emezi, Ilo, Ngamije) — all of which use African language code-switching as a deliberate political act — this story uses Swahili, French, and Lingala similarly. Is the current balance right, or is there a case for more untranslated language left to stand on its own?
> > 9. New (2026-07-23): a first Lingala/Swahili lexicon pass added seven touches — Morisho's "Ndeko," the mother's "Bolingo," D'juma's "Nzambe," a fourth boda boda catcall ("Kitoko mingi"), "sebene"/"atalaku" in the Fally Ipupa moment, Morisho's whispered "Keba" to Djamela, Djamela's "Motema" breaking through her own deflection, and an overheard "Ezali makasi" in the police station. All pulled from [[Language Lexicon]], avoiding terms flagged there as Kinshasa-specific. Does the density feel right, too light, or already too much for one pass?
> 
> ---
> 
> ## REVISION CHECKLIST
> 
> - [ ] Opening: Daudi's voice consistent throughout?
> - [ ] Djamela's name withheld at the stop — dread working?
> - [ ] Cologne: named or unnamed in opening?
> - [ ] D'juma's lake speech: appears twice (here + end)?
> - [ ] The twenty seconds: echo between rain scene and Jean Luc scene clear?
> - [ ] Morisho: suspicious but not obviously guilty?
> - [ ] Jean Luc: credible threat, not cartoon villain?
> - [ ] Police station: Daudi's reaction to pregnancy reveal present?
> - [ ] Thin envelope: followed by at least one more beat?
> - [ ] Entropy motif: tracked across all scenes?
> - [ ] Lexicon pass (2026-07-23): does each inserted Lingala/Swahili word still read as earned on a re-read, or does any of the seven feel bolted on?

> ---
> tags: [kalemie, draft, djamela, story-cycle, prose, barrett]
> story: Kalemie
> aliases: [Chantal Draft 2, Chantal Barrett Pass]
> date: 2026-07-26

> supersedes: "2026-07-26 — Chantal (Djamela POV Draft)"
> pov: Djamela, first person
> words: ~3720
> ---
> 
> # CHANTAL
> 
> > [!NOTE] What changed from Draft 1, and why
> > Draft 1 is kept at [[2026-07-26 — Chantal (Djamela POV Draft)]]. This pass runs the whole story against [[Short Story Writing by Charles Raymond Barrett|Barrett]]. Almost every change is a cut.
> >
> > — **The aphorism rate is down by roughly two-thirds.** Barrett's warning about the first-person narrator who "prefers moralizing and philosophizing to the legitimate furthering of the plot" ([[Methods of Narration — The Author Must Vanish]]) and his rule that a story saddled with a moral "ceases to be a story and becomes an argument" ([[The Art of Omission — Unity, Padding, and Proportion]]) both land on the same sentences [[The Funhouse Mirror — AI Output as Cliché Detector|the mirror test]] flagged. Draft 1 closed a paragraph with a neat inversion roughly twenty times. This one does it five.
> > — **Sentence rhythm rebalanced.** Draft 1 ran 63% of sentences under ten words — past Cleland's guidance and into what Barrett calls the "bumpety-bump" style. Target here is nearer 50%, with more medium declaratives carrying the interior passages.
> > — **The ending now stops at the act.** Barrett: climax and conclusion should be as nearly simultaneous as possible, and the climax proper runs about six words. Draft 1 ended on three paragraphs of reflection about the lake. This one ends on what she does.
> > — **Morisho's fate is paid off.** Barrett requires the climax to decide the destinies of *all* characters and the fate of all their schemes. Neither previous draft disposed of the innocent man arrested for her death. She now foresees it — which costs 40 words and is the sharpest thing in the story.
> > — **Two characters consolidated.** Alicia and Béatrice merged into one girl. [[The Isolation Principle — One Emotion, One Incident|Barrett's ceiling is two speaking parts]]; this does not reach it, but it stops adding.
> > — **Title kept.** "Chantal" passes all five of [[Titles Good and Bad|Barrett's title tests]]; "Kalemie" fails three.
> 
> ---
> 
> Nobody tells you the exact day your body stops being only yours. Mine picked a Tuesday.
> 
> I was behind the school gate counting on my fingers, the way you count coins when you already know they are not enough. Alicia was talking about somebody's hairdo. Thirty-four days. I counted twice, and the first time I got thirty-three, and I wanted the smaller number to be the true one, so I counted again and it got bigger.
> 
> That is not how counting is supposed to work.
> 
> The bell went. Alicia took my arm the way she does and I let her, and we walked in, and I sat down at a desk with a wobble in it and opened a book about the digestive system of a cow.
> 
> I am telling you about the Tuesday first because everybody wants to start with the man. The man comes later. The man is not the beginning of anything except his own story.
> 
> ---
> 
> We live in Mihogoni, the down part of town, where the road stops being a road and becomes a suggestion. My father sells fruit on Ave de Tanganyique in the mornings and lays his hands on people in the afternoons, and both of these are jobs, and neither is the kind you say out loud at school.
> 
> In Primary Six, Alicia told a whole row that our house smelled of fish, back when she was somebody else's friend and not mine. Everybody laughed. I laughed too, because that is the fastest way to stop being the thing people are laughing at.
> 
> Our house does smell of fish. That is not the part that stayed with me.
> 
> There are children in this town we call the ghost kids. They go to Monseigneur André Kaseba and they wear checkered skirts with ties and blazers and they sit in the back of their parents' cars looking at nothing. When our dala dala stops beside one of those cars at the lights on Ave de Industrielle, we look in and they do not look out. Not because they are proud. Because we are not there.
> 
> Morisho hangs off the side of the dala dala and shouts mia tano mia tano until his voice goes. He told us once that those children are ghosts to us, that we will never see them or talk to them. He meant it as a warning. I heard it as a price list.
> 
> ---
> 
> I turned thirteen in March and my father bought a watermelon.
> 
> Not a cake. A watermelon, with one candle pushed into the top of it, standing crooked because a watermelon is not built to hold a candle, which anybody could have told him.
> 
> Baba, I said, you could have got a cake. Even a cheap one.
> 
> Watermelons are healthier, he said. More natural. More African. Cakes are a western thing.
> 
> My mother said watermelons are sweet, like you, Djamela, in the voice she uses when she is helping him be right about something.
> 
> I want to be fair to him, because I have not always been. He is not a stupid man. He was handed a town that had been emptied out twice before he got to it, and he has nothing to give me except proverbs and fruit, and he decided that if he refused the world outside Kalemie hard enough I would stop wanting it. It did not work like that. It only made the wanting private.
> 
> Make a wish, he said. Blow.
> 
> I closed my eyes and said: to be Kim Kardashian, amen.
> 
> What is Kim Kardashian, my father said.
> 
> A social media influencer, my mother said, fast, the way you move a knife off a table.
> 
> I blew the candle out. The smoke went up and sat under the iron roof and did not leave.
> 
> Later I sat on the porch with Daudi and looked at the lake with the rusted ships in it. He thinks I want things, I said.
> 
> Daudi said nothing, which is what Daudi does.
> 
> I do want things, I said. I want to control them. I want to conquer, the way the Europeans did, except now you do not need weapons or degrees. You only need followers.
> 
> I was thirteen years and one hour old and I believed all of it. Nobody made me believe it. I worked it out myself, from the evidence in front of me, which was a town where the people with cars had taken things and the people without cars had been taken from.
> 
> ---
> 
> Daudi is the boy from two houses down. He is a year and a half older than me and he has a wall in his room where he nails things he has found. A hubcap. The skull of an antelope. A bicycle wheel I am fairly sure he did not find.
> 
> He calls it a collection. Everybody else calls it rubbish.
> 
> His mother died having him, which is a thing that gets said about him rather than by him, and he has built a whole woman out of that absence — patient, soft, never once disappointed in him, because a woman who never existed does not have to be. He has never said one word of it to me. I know it the way you know the shape of a room in the dark.
> 
> When we were small I gave him a motherboard.
> 
> I found it behind the internet café that used to be by the market, in a heap of things that had stopped working. It was green, with little gold roads on it going nowhere, and I thought it was the most beautiful broken thing I had ever seen. I gave it to him because he was the only person I knew who would understand that a thing can be beautiful after it stops being useful.
> 
> He nailed it to the wall between the skull and the hubcap. He has never once mentioned where he got it. I do not think he remembers it was me. Daudi thinks he found everything.
> 
> ---
> 
> The car was a BMW, black, with windows you could not see into.
> 
> We had got off the dala dala at the dagaa stop and the boda boda men were doing what boda boda men do, which is call you beautiful in a way that is not a compliment but a notice — a way of saying that everyone has agreed you are available for comment now.
> 
> Then a horn, different from theirs. The window came down with a sound I had never heard a window make. Not the clink of a handle going round. A swish, like a word said quietly on purpose.
> 
> Je m'appelle Jean Luc, he said. Tu es d'où, ma belle.
> 
> I want to tell this part exactly, because everybody who has heard it since has decided they already know.
> 
> I said: Rue Saint Michel.
> 
> I lied immediately and easily and without deciding to. Rue Saint Michel is where the ghost children live and I have never set foot on it, and it came out of my mouth as though it had been sitting behind my teeth for years, waiting.
> 
> Daudi started saying the thing about strangers. Mama alisema —
> 
> We shouldn't talk to strangers, said Jean Luc, finishing it for him. And he smiled.
> 
> I have thought about that smile more than about anything else. He knew what he was. He said it out loud, in a joke, in front of both of us, and he was so certain it would cost him nothing that he was right.
> 
> He told us he was a director. Consul général de Belgique. He said it in French with Lingala underneath, and his French was worse than he thought it was, and I noticed, and I decided not to have noticed.
> 
> That is the part I would like written down somewhere. Not that I was fooled. That I did the deciding.
> 
> He wrote a phone number on a piece of paper and put a ten thousand note under it. My eyes went wide and Daudi saw them go wide and has never asked me why. It was not the money. Ten thousand is a school skirt. It was that a man with a car like that had stopped it, in the road, in front of the boda boda men, for me.
> 
> Je m'appelle, he asked.
> 
> Chantal, I said.
> 
> Nobody had asked me for a name before. They had only ever used the one I came with. He handed me that name the way you hand somebody a gift, and I took it the way you take a gift, and it was a collar with a good buckle.
> 
> Daudi stood beside me the whole time and did not say one word after mama alisema. I did not look at him. I could feel the hurt coming off him like heat off a wall and I stood in it and warmed my hands, because a girl who has just been chosen wants somebody there to see her be chosen.
> 
> I was thirteen. I am not asking to be forgiven for that. I am asking for it to be counted correctly.
> 
> ---
> 
> It rained in April, hard, the way it does, and I ran to Daudi's house because it was closer than mine.
> 
> His room is one room. Baked brick, a window the size of two hands, a foam mattress on the floor with a grey sheet, and buckets set out under the places where the roof has given up. He had physics papers spread across the mattress and he moved them for me without being asked.
> 
> Do you understand entropy, I said.
> 
> I knew what it was. I had heard him say it eleven times. I asked because I wanted him to be the person who knew something, for once, in front of me.
> 
> It is a natural state, he said. Order needs energy. Disorder is what happens when you stop.
> 
> My father says the lake is the opposite, I said. He says it puts things back in order. He says it remembers everything in its right place.
> 
> Your father is a wise man, Daudi said. Then, a minute later: it is hydrogen and oxygen. It has no intelligence.
> 
> We sat there and the rain came through the roof and hit the buckets and the sound filled the room so completely that there was nothing else in it. My knee was touching the edge of his knee. I had put it there.
> 
> Then what do you want to remember, Mr Scientist, I said. Encrypted data? Binary codes? Or a colourful moment with no meaning in it?
> 
> He did not answer and he did not move. I watched a boy who wanted something more than he has wanted anything hold completely still, because moving might have cost him the version of me he kept in his head.
> 
> So I kissed him.
> 
> It lasted about twenty seconds. I counted, because I count everything.
> 
> Then I pulled back and said, see? Not everything falls apart.
> 
> He looked at me as though I had shown him the back door of the universe.
> 
> That kiss cost me nothing at all. It was the cheapest thing I have ever given anybody and he has carried it around ever since like the down payment on a house, and I let him, and I knew exactly what he thought it was while he was thinking it.
> 
> ---
> 
> I am not going to describe the hotel.
> 
> Here is what I have instead, which is the inventory, because that is how I have always understood the world.
> 
> A key fob shaped like a lion, cartoon, plastic, the kind you would give a child. It opens a door on the third floor. I keep it in my left pocket where the seam has gone and never in my right.
> 
> A perfume. Shalis by Remy Marquis. Not mine. It gets into the weave of a khanga and will not come out with soap, only with time, so I learned to leave certain clothes at the bottom of the basket for four days before my mother does the wash.
> 
> Fingernails, done every Friday and taken off every Monday before school, because at school there are rules and at the hotel there are different rules, and I live between the two of them like somebody crossing a road that never stops.
> 
> A photograph on my phone. Me, and at the edge of the frame a hand on my shoulder with a gold ring on it. The ring is not real gold. He wears it like a crown. I tagged it blessed and forty-one people liked it and none of them asked me anything.
> 
> Two vaccinations, low on the arm, near the elbow, not where they usually put them.
> 
> Anybody who wanted to could have read that. It was all on the outside of me for months.
> 
> ---
> 
> We went to Ave de Bobozo about the car.
> 
> I say the car. There had been a car, and it came off the road at the corner near the market and did not stop, and my leg has a mark on it going yellow at the edges. That was true. I want you to know that one of the things I said that year was true.
> 
> Daudi came because I did not want to go alone. He said it was because his bicycle was stolen once, so he knew the forms.
> 
> The station smelled of dust and men. A woman in a black dress was explaining to an officer that it was not prostitution, it was escort services, they had cards with a company number on them. He said, more like your pimp's number. Everybody laughed except her and me.
> 
> The officer who took us was young and typing with one finger, hunting each letter down as though it owed him something. He looked up at my face and made a sound in his throat. Mhh. What do we have here.
> 
> Daudi said, she is reporting a hit and run.
> 
> So why are you talking, the officer said. You sound like you know more than she does. And he winked at Daudi, one man to another, over the top of me.
> 
> He asked where and what type of car and who was driving, typing at the speed of a man who has already decided what this is worth. So I held out my phone and showed him the photograph with the hand and the ring, because I had worked out on the way there that a hit and run was worth nothing and a man like that was worth something.
> 
> We deal with hard evidence, he said. Not AI. Not phantom ghosts.
> 
> I leaned close enough to smell onion and whiskey on him, and backed off, and said it.
> 
> My menstrual cycle. There is a latency of more than a month.
> 
> The air in that room changed as though a door had opened somewhere.
> 
> The older one came over, big gut on him, the kind you get from a lifetime of small envelopes and large drinks, and he started reciting. The penal code. A teenage minor. Rape. Thirty years and twelve lashes on top. He said it the way the priest at St Augustine says the parts he has said ten thousand times. A buccal swab will suffice as conclusive evidence. Shame on the predator, for luring young girls.
> 
> The young one kept typing. Then, quietly: where is his number. We will call him. We will summon him now. Yes. We will finish the case, if you understand me.
> 
> I understood him perfectly. I have understood men like him since I was eleven, when my auntie's neighbour let his hand sit too long on the back of my chair while a room full of adults agreed together not to see it, the way you decide not to see a crack in a wall you are only renting.
> 
> I gave him the phone. I dialled the number myself.
> 
> Jean Luc took twenty minutes. Twenty minutes means he was already in town, already near, already somewhere he could reach us from — which means that the whole time I had been walking around thinking I was choosing things, I had been the thing being watched.
> 
> He came in with his shoulders back and a fat envelope under his jacket and he did not look surprised and he did not look afraid. He looked like a man whose phone had finally rung.
> 
> I saw one thing. When the older officer said the word minor, something went across Jean Luc's face and was gone. A flicker, like a bulb deciding whether to hold. He had planned for envelopes. He had not planned for consequences. Then it closed over and he smiled and it was as though it had never been there.
> 
> They took us to the room at the back.
> 
> Nothing happened to my body in that room. What happened is that three men worked out what I was worth, out loud, in two languages, in front of me, using the word compensation, and then agreed on it.
> 
> I sat on a plastic chair with a crack in it and did the arithmetic along with them, because I cannot help it, I have never been able to help it. And the number they landed on was smaller than the one I had been carrying in my head for eight months.
> 
> I came out of that room bent over. Daudi saw me come out and I know what he thought, because he is fifteen and good and he thinks shame is the only thing that can bend a person.
> 
> It was not shame. It was arithmetic.
> 
> They gave me the envelope on the way out. It had been fat going in. It was thin coming out, and nobody said anything about that, and nobody had to.
> 
> ---
> 
> School on the Monday. I took the nails off on the Sunday night, the same as always.
> 
> Here is the thing I have worked out and cannot put down. When this finishes — and it is going to finish, I have known that since the twenty minutes — somebody will be taken for it. Not him. Him they will call as a witness, if they call him at all. They will take somebody visible and poor who cannot afford an envelope, and the whole town will agree to be satisfied, and I think it will be Morisho, because he is the one who shouts on the road every morning where everybody can see him.
> 
> He gave me a coin off my fare once and called me shemeji. That is the whole of what he did.
> 
> I went to my locker at the end of the day when the corridor was empty. There was a good coat of dust on the inside of the door and I wrote his name in it with one finger.
> 
> Daudi.
> 
> It is a short name. It does not take long to write and it does not take long to look at.
> 
> Then I wiped it off with the side of my hand, because a name in dust is evidence, and everything I have learned this year I have learned about what happens to people when there is evidence lying around with their name on it.
> 
> On the way home I stopped at his house. He was out. His father was in and his father does not look at me, which suited me.
> 
> I went into the room with the buckets and took the motherboard down off its nail.
> 
> There is a board under the third bucket that comes up if you know it comes up. I have known since I was nine. I put the fob under it — the lion, cartoon, plastic, the kind you would give a child — and I put the board back, and I stood on it, and it did not creak.
> 
> Then I hung the motherboard on its nail, straight, the way he likes it, between the skull and the hubcap.
> 
> He will find it. Not soon. But he is a boy who takes things apart, and one day he will be in that room with nothing to do.
> 
> I walked home along the shore counting the ships. There are four of them and there have always been four and they have not moved in my lifetime and they will not move in his. It came out even, the way it does.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## AFTERWORD — BARRETT AUDIT
> 
> *Measured against [[Short Story Writing by Charles Raymond Barrett|Barrett]] and the prior draft. Prose only.*
> 
> | Metric | Draft 2 | Draft 1 | Master draft | Standard |
> | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
> | Prose words | **3,399** | 4,099 | 3,941 | 2,000–5,000 gate; Barrett's ideal 3,000–5,000 |
> | Sentences | 267 | 320 | 284 | — |
> | Average length | 12.8 words | 12.8 | 14.0 | Cleland: under 20 |
> | Sentences under 10 words | **160 (60%)** | 63% | 42% | Barrett: excess = "bumpety-bump" |
> | Sentences over 30 words | 22 (8%) | 11% | 5% | Barrett: long = interiority |
> | Perception verbs | 22 | 26 | 26 | Cleland: cut on sight |
> | Closed aphorisms | **5** | ~20 | — | Barrett: a story that argues stops being a story |
> | Words after the climax | **~45** | ~180 | n/a | Barrett: climax and conclusion as near simultaneous as possible |
> | Destinies settled | **All, incl. Morisho** | Morisho unresolved | Morisho unresolved | Barrett: the climax must decide all |
> | Headroom under 5,000 | **1,601 words** | 901 | 1,059 | prize ceiling |
> 
> > [!WARNING] Two claims in this note were wrong before measurement
> > This table was first written asserting ~3,720 words and ~50% short sentences. The real figures are **3,399** and **60%**. Both are recorded above as measured. The discipline is [[The Cold Read Protocol|the vault's own]]: an assessment containing no verified number is noise.
> >
> > **The sentence-rhythm fix substantially failed.** Draft 1 ran 63% of sentences under ten words; Draft 2 runs 60%. That is a three-point move where the intent was ten, and it means Barrett's bumpety-bump objection is still live. The cuts removed whole *paragraphs* of commentary rather than rebalancing rhythm inside surviving sentences — which is why the word count fell 700 while the ratio barely moved. **This is the outstanding job on the next pass**, and it is a read-aloud job, not a counting one.
> 
> **What Barrett bought this draft:**
> 
> - **The Morisho foresight** is the single best addition and it came directly from [[Climax and Conclusion — The Story in Six Words|his rule about settling all destinies]]. It costs 40 words, pays the debt both earlier drafts left, and makes her the only person in the story who sees the machine clearly — which is the characterisation the whole piece is built on.
> - **The ending stops at the act.** The lake paragraphs are gone. The last image is a girl counting four ships that have not moved and will not move, which carries the theme without stating it — [[The Art of Omission — Unity, Padding, and Proportion|his rule that the soul of the story sits under the surface]], not on it.
> - **Roughly 380 words came out**, almost all of it commentary. Per [[The Balance Test — Length as a Function of Idea]], a shorter story that has arrived beats a longer one still travelling — and it opens ~1,280 words of headroom under the prize ceiling.
> 
> **Where it still fails Barrett, knowingly:**
> 
> - **Speaking parts: six.** His ceiling is two. Not fixable without gutting the world.
> - **Time and place unities: broken.** A year, six locations. Held together by voice and single action, which is the modern solution he did not have.
> - **First person.** He advises against it — but his central objection, that the narrator has come safely through, is void here. She has not. See [[Methods of Narration — The Author Must Vanish]].
> 
> **Open questions:**
> 
> 1. Is the Morisho passage in the right place? It currently sits after the envelope and before the locker. It could open the story instead, which would make the whole piece an explanation of a prediction.
> 2. Barrett wants the climax proper in about six words. Candidate here: *"I put the board back, and I stood on it."* Test whether the ships paragraph dilutes it.
> 3. Five aphorisms may still be one too many. The weakest is likely *"It was a collar with a good buckle."*
> 4. If this becomes the primary submission rather than Kalemie, the motherboard reveal must be cut from Kalemie — only one story can hold it.
> ```

> ```
